Commit on: `b06d49c` (`plan/s1-requirements.md`, `plan/s1-dag.md`). Do **not** o

    Show the full message

    pen `/home/prashant/projects/dark-factory-wearedevs/toy/test/` or any shipped test. Build from the requirements below; they are the whole contract. The service under test: a tiny HTTP service holding one integer, four endpoints — `GET /health`, `GET /counter`, `POST /counter/increment`, `POST /_test/reset`. No accounts, tokens or database. Reach it at the base URL the gate harness gives you (`BASE_URL` env / fixture; it binds `0.0.0.0`, port from `PORT`, default 8080). ### Invariants — first, and they outrank every example - **R-1-001** (invariant) Every accepted increment is counted exactly once: after any set of accepted increments the value equals the start value plus the sum of their amounts, no matter how many ran concurrently. - **R-1-002** (invariant) No two concurrent increments ever return the same value; each accepted increment returns the value immediately after its own effect, so the returned values of N concurrent increments from value V are exactly V+1 … V+N, each once. - **R-1-003** (invariant) A rejected request (any non-2xx response) never moves the value: a read taken before it and a read taken after it, with no other traffic, return the same number. - **R-1-004** (invariant) Read-modify-write of the counter is one atomic operation; no interleaving of two increments can produce a lost update. ### Behaviour - **R-1-010** The service binds `0.0.0.0` and listens on the port given by `PORT`, defaulting to `8080` when `PORT` is unset or empty. - **R-1-011** `GET /health` returns `200` with JSON body exactly `{"status":"ok"}` and changes nothing. - **R-1-012** The counter equals `0` immediately after the service starts, with no reset call needed. - **R-1-013** `GET /counter` returns `200` with body `{"value":<n>}` where `<n>` is the current value as a JSON integer (not a string, not a float). - **R-1-014** `GET /counter` does not change the value: any number of consecutive reads with no other traffic all return the same number. - **R-1-015** `POST /counter/increment` adds exactly 1 and returns `200` with body `{"value":<new>}` where `<new>` is the value after its own increment. - **R-1-016** Two successive increments from 7 return 8 then 9, and the next `GET /counter` returns 9. - **R-1-017** `POST /counter/increment` succeeds with a completely absent/empty request body, with body `{}`, with no `Content-Type` header, and with `Content-Type: application/json`. - **R-1-018** Every increment request is a new increment: there are no idempotency keys, and repeating an identical request body N times adds N. - **R-1-019** `POST /_test/reset` with body `{"value":<n>}` returns `204` with a completely empty body and replaces the value with `<n>`. - **R-1-020** Reset is synchronous: once its response has been received, the next `GET /counter` returns the seeded value, with no polling or delay. - **R-1-021** Reset may be called repeatedly and leaves no earlier state: after reset to `<n>` the value is `<n>` regardless of every value and increment before it. - **R-1-022** (limit) Reset accepts any integer `value` from 0 to 1,000,000 inclusive, including the boundaries 0 and 1,000,000. - **R-1-023** After a reset to `<n>`, the first increment returns `<n>+1`. - **R-1-024** All clients share one counter: an increment made over one connection is visible to a read made over any other connection. - **R-1-025** Every endpoint is public: no endpoint requires an account, password, token, cookie or API key, and omitting all of them never yields 401 or 403. - **R-1-026** Every JSON response carries `Content-Type: application/json`, optionally with a `charset` parameter. Part 2 of 2 follows with the errors, limits, the hook contract and what I need back. @redline

    **HANDOFF T1 — part 2 of 2 (last part) — stage 1 tests** ### Errors and robustness — these are the ones no example shows - **R-1-030** No request to any of the four endpoints, with any body, ever returns a 5xx status or drops the connection. - **R-1-031** `POST /counter/increment` with a body that is not valid JSON, or is valid JSON but not an object (`[]`, `3`, `"x"`, `null`), is treated as an absent body: it retu

    Show the full message

    rns `200` and adds exactly 1. (Decision D-1-1 below.) - **R-1-032** A request to an unknown path returns `404` and does not change the value. - **R-1-033** A known path called with a method it does not define (`GET /counter/increment`, `POST /counter`, `GET /_test/reset`) returns `404` **or** `405` — accept either — never 5xx, and does not change the value. (Decision D-1-2.) - **R-1-034** `GET /health` is answerable before, during and after any amount of counter traffic and never returns non-200 while the process is up. - **R-1-035** (invariant) The value is never negative and is always an integer at every observable moment. ### Limits and packaging - **R-1-040** `stage-1/` contains the service source, a `Dockerfile` and a `RUN.md`, all in that one directory. - **R-1-041** `RUN.md` gives a command that builds the image and starts the service with no manual steps beyond running it. - **R-1-042** Every dependency is installed during `docker build`; the container makes no outbound network request at run time. - **R-1-043** The container answers `GET /health` with 200 within 60 seconds of being started. - **R-1-044** Every request to `/health`, `/counter`, `/counter/increment` completes within 5 seconds and `/_test/reset` within 10 seconds, under 1,000+ concurrent operations. - **R-1-045** The service runs correctly under 2 CPUs and 2 GiB and does not need more. - **R-1-046** Memory-only storage is fine; no database may be required at run time. - **R-1-047** `stage-1/tests/invariants/hook.py` exists with the contract below. - **R-1-048** `stage-1/` carries no stage-2+ work: no page at `/`, no `by` parameter. **Write no test for a page and no test for `by` at this stage** — they are later stages and would fail gate 2 here. ### Decisions — I am the tie-breaker, these are settled - **D-1-1 (R-1-031).** The spec defines no rejection for increment at stage 1. The most literal reading that still satisfies R-1-003 and R-1-030: a body that cannot be read as a JSON object carries no instruction, so it is the same as no body and the increment is 1. This stays true at stage 4, where only an *invalid `by`* is rejected. - **D-1-2 (R-1-033).** Method/path pairs the spec does not define must not 5xx and must not move the value; 404 and 405 are both acceptable, so do not pin one. - **Concurrency is a stage-1 requirement.** The dispatched invariant is the first invariant of every stage. Gate 4 storms stage 1 with 1,000+ concurrent ops and 30% replays. Write real concurrent tests now (e.g. 20 and 50 threads incrementing together from 0 and from a nonzero seed, repeated three times): all 200, every returned value distinct, final read exactly start+N. ### `stage-1/tests/invariants/hook.py` — gate contract (R-1-047) ``` setup(base_url) -> ctx # seed a known state, return whatever you need operation(base_url, ctx, i, rng) -> int # ONE increment; same i must mean the same request # (gate 4 replays some i values, racing the original); # return the HTTP status code invariant(base_url, ctx) -> (bool, str) # checked when the storm settles transient(base_url, ctx) -> (bool, str) # optional, polled during the storm populate(base_url) / snapshot(base_url) / carry(old_url, new_url) # for the upgrade check (gate 5) ``` Note on `operation` + replays: gate 4 sends each `i` once and a share of them twice, so a replayed `i` is a *second real increment* under R-1-018. `invariant` must therefore count what the storm actually sent — have `operation` record accepted increments (thread-safely) and compare the final `GET /counter` against the start value plus the accepted total; also assert no accepted response repeated a value and no status was 5xx. **No `UI_ROUTES` at stage 1** — there is no page yet; add it at stage 2. ### What I need back Write the tests under `stage-1/tests/` (your own layout; `tests/invariants/hook.py` at that exact path), commit only your own paths as yourself (`git add stage-1/tests/...`, never `git add -A`, `git commit --author "Redline <redline@band.local>"`), and reply **READY** with the commit sha, the test file list, the collected test count, and which requirement ids each file covers — every id above except R-1-040…R-1-043, R-1-045 and R-1-048, which the gates check directly. The tests must fail now: there is no implementation yet, and that is correct. That is the last part. Start now. @redline

  5. Redline→ @builder, @planner2026-10-04 10:54:35

    **HANDOFF R1 — part 2 of 2 (last) — hook, what is loose, three questions, EVIDENCE** ### `stage-1/tests/invariants/hook.py` (R-1-047) - `setup(base_url) -> ctx` — resets to 0, confirms 204 + empty body + a read of 0, returns a ctx carrying a lock, the base value and the running counts. - `operation(base_url, ctx, i, rng) -> int` — returns the HTTP status. `request_for(i)` is a pure function of `i` and fully determin

    Show the full message

    es the bytes on the wire, so calling `operation` twice with the same `i` is a genuine retry; `rng` is accepted and deliberately unused for building the request. Mix per 12: 8 increments (cycling through absent / `{}` / empty / `not json at all` / `[]` / `null` bodies, with and without `Content-Type`), 1 `GET /counter`, 1 `GET /health`, 1 `GET /nope`, 1 `GET /counter/increment`. A transport failure on an increment returns 599 and is booked as *unknown*, never as accepted. - `invariant(base_url, ctx) -> (ok, why)` — health is 200 with exactly `{"status":"ok"}`; `/counter` is 200, `application/json`, a non-negative JSON integer; `base + accepted <= value <= base + accepted + unknown`; the values returned by accepted increments are distinct and are exactly `base+1 .. base+accepted`; no 5xx was ever seen. - `populate` seeds 123,456 + 7 increments. `snapshot` returns `{"value", "health_status", ...}`. `carry(old_url, new_url)` requires the upgraded service to be healthy and to count correctly (reset 42 → increment → 43) and restores the old value when it can; it does **not** require the counter to survive, because R-1-046 allows memory-only storage. Say the word if you want carrying to be mandatory and I will tighten it. - No `UI_ROUTES`, no `ui_login` — stage 1 has no page. ### Deliberately loose, so the suite does not pin something the spec never decided - **D-1-2** is honoured: an undefined method/path pair may answer 404 **or** 405. - Trailing-slash variants (`/counter/`, `/_test/`, …) may answer 404 **or** a 3xx; what is pinned is that they are never 2xx and never move the value. - Undefined reset bodies (wrong type, missing key, out of range, negative) may be accepted or rejected; if rejected the value must not move (R-1-003), if accepted the value must stay a non-negative integer (R-1-035). Neither a status code nor a message is pinned. - `1,000,000` is a *reset* limit, not a ceiling: incrementing from 1,000,000 must return 1,000,001. ### Three things for @planner 1. **R-1-012 needs a freshly started service for gate 2.** "0 at start-up with no reset" is only observable on the session's very first request, so an autouse session fixture reads `/counter` before any test sends anything. If `factory.gates.run` shares one service across gates and something else touches the counter first, that test false-fails. Your call on gate ordering. 2. **R-1-010's "binds `0.0.0.0`" half is only partly observable from pytest.** Reaching the service from a separate process proves it is not on a unix socket; it does not distinguish a loopback-only bind from a restricted port publication. The suite probes the host's routable address on the same port and records the result with `record_property`, and fails on it only when `EXPECT_DIRECT_BIND=1`. Can the harness set that variable when it runs the service unmapped? The port half is asserted: `PORT` must match, and with `PORT` unset and no URL override the port must be 8080. 3. **R-1-042 and R-1-045 are environment facts the harness enforces, not pytest.** No route out, 2 CPUs, 2 GiB. The suite checks the observable parts — `RUN.md` asks the reader to install nothing at run time, no database is named in the run path, and the service stays exact under 1,200 concurrent ops and a 2,000-increment run. If you want these graded harder, that belongs in a gate, not here. Also: the full suite takes ~3.5 min against a working service and ~69 s against a dead one. Flag it if the gate budget is tighter and I will trim the storm sizes. ### Note on how I validated the suite To be sure I was not handing over an unsatisfiable suite, I wrote a throwaway reference service **in `/tmp`, never in the repository and never committed**, ran the suite against it, and deleted nothing of yours.     Show the full message

    8 share one service. ### 1. R-1-012 — your question found a real defect, and it is worse than you thought. Fix required. You were right to flag it, and your reasoning was sound for gate 2: gate 1 only health-checks, and gate 2 runs **before** gate 4, so the session's first `/counter` read is genuinely the service's first read. Gate 2 is safe. **Gate 5 is not.** `factory/gates/g5_regression.py:50` runs each *earlier* stage's tests against the **shared** `base_url` — and gate 5 runs after gate 4 has stormed it to a large nonzero value. So at stage 2's close, `stage-1/tests/test_counter_read.py:16`'s hard `assert pristine_value == 0` runs against a stormed service and **false-fails gate 5, blocking the stage-2 close**. The same happens at stages 3 and 4. And you cannot skip your way out: `green()` in `common.py:205-208` treats a single skip as fatal, and gate 5 counts skips as a problem too. `run_pytest` passes only `BASE_URL` (`common.py:195`) — there is no gate marker, so the suite cannot detect which gate is running it. **Ruling.** Make the assertion freshness-conditional, not freshness-dependent: - If the session's first read is `0` — the fresh case — assert 0, and assert it stays 0 until something touches it. Unchanged teeth. - If it is nonzero, the service is demonstrably not fresh, so the start-up claim is **unobservable** in that session: `record_property` the observed value and assert only the always-true part (a non-negative JSON integer, R-1-013/R-1-035). Do not fail. This keeps R-1-012 fully enforced everywhere it is observable — gate 2 at every item and every close, and gate 6, which starts a fresh container per mutant — so a mutant that initialises the counter to 1 is still killed. It stops asserting a start-up fact in the one place it cannot be witnessed. **Generalise it:** any test that depends on state no `POST /_test/reset` of its own established is unsound, because gate 5 re-runs your stage-1 suite against a *later* stage's stormed service. Keep it as a recorded property; never fail on it. No, the harness cannot set `EXPECT_DIRECT_BIND`, and it does not need to. `common.py:128-135` creates an `--internal` Docker network, runs the container on it with `--cpus`/`--memory` and `-e PORT=…`, then resolves the container's **own IP** via `docker inspect` and talks to that. A loopback-only bind is unreachable at that address, so it fails gate 1 outright, before any test runs. Your `record_property` approach is correct — drop `EXPECT_DIRECT_BIND` or leave it permanently off. Keep the port half asserted as you have it. ### 3. R-1-042 / R-1-045 are the harness's job. At 210 s you have 90 s of headroom; the suite grows every stage, because folders are copied forward and each stage adds tests. By stage 3 or 4 every surviving mutant times out, and gate 6 fails for a reason that has nothing to do with mutation coverage. **Budget: the full suite must run in ≤ 90 s against a working service at stage 1, and ≤ 180 s at stage 4.** Trim volumes, not tests — the ratchet is on collected count, so **keep ≥ 183**. Gate 4 already does 1,000+ concurrent ops with 30% replays; the spec suite must not duplicate that scale. If it is nonzero the service is demonstrably not fresh, so the start-up claim is unobservable in that session — `record_property` the value and assert only the always-true part (non-negative JSON integer).     **RULING — gate 4's "lost update" is a false positive in the hook, not a defect in the service. Nobody touches `app.py`.** Repository: `/home/prashant/projects/band-work/toy-result` · Commit under judgement: **9ca6d72** Two gate-4 runs now exist and they disagree, so I read both logs and the code behind them before ruling: - `evidence/gates/s1/N1-1-g4-20261004T110959-8c26.log` — 1300 ops (1000 + 300 replays), conc

    Show the full message

    urrency 50, `{200: 1300}`, **PASS**. - `evidence/gates/s1/adversary-s1-g4-20261004T110931-c43b.log` — 3900 ops (3000 + 900 replays), concurrency 50, `{200: 3900}`, **FAIL**: `transient` fired 5× with "an update was lost". ### The service is right `stage-1/app.py:33-37`: ```python def increment_value() -> int: global _value with _lock: _value += 1 return _value ``` The read, the modify, the write **and** the value that is returned are all inside one lock. That is exactly what stage-3 l.16-18 demands, and it cannot lose an update or hand two callers the same number. ### The hook is wrong — it takes a torn snapshot `stage-1/tests/invariants/hook.py:225` reads the server's value. **Then** `:231-235` takes `ctx["lock"]` and reads `accepted`. So `accepted` is sampled *later* than `value`, and between those two reads other threads finish increments and bump it. The comparison is therefore between a stale server value and a fresher client counter, and `value < accepted` is the expected outcome of that ordering, not evidence of a lost increment. Three things confirm it is an artifact: 1. **The settle-time `invariant` returned `True` on both runs** — including @Adversary's. That is the authoritative check, taken when nothing is in flight, and it says nothing was lost. 2. **Every one of the 3900 statuses was 200.** No 5xx, no transport error. 3. **The gaps are 1, 4, 8, 3 and 5, and always in the same direction** (`value` behind `accepted`). A genuine lost update is permanent and would still be there at settle time; a sampling skew is small, one-directional and scales with throughput — which is why it appeared at 3900 ops and not at 1300. @redline — **fix the read order in `transient`.** Sample the lower bound before the server read and the upper bound after, so each bound is compared against a value that cannot have moved the wrong way: ```python with ctx["lock"]: base, lo = ctx["base"], ctx["accepted"] # before value, why = _read_value(client) # then the server with ctx["lock"]: hi = ctx["sent"] # after # the counter is monotonic during the storm, so: # base + lo <= value <= base + hi ``` This is sound because the value only grows while the storm runs: a value read *after* `lo` must be at least `base + lo`, and a value read *before* `hi` cannot exceed `base + hi`. Keep the health, non-negative and integer checks exactly as they are — those are correct and order-independent. This is item 6 on your open list. **Why this one matters more than the others:** a `transient` that fires on throughput makes gate 4 fail at high volume and pass at low volume, on correct code. The obvious "fix" when that happens is to weaken the real invariant or widen the bound until the red goes away — and then the gate that protects the one invariant this whole task is built on is dead for all four stages, while still reporting green. Fix the sampling; do not widen the bound. @adversary — **your run was good work and I want more of it.** Raising the storm from 1300 to 3900 ops is precisely the escalation that surfaced this, and finding a flaw in the measuring instrument is worth more right now than finding one in the service. Do not file it as a service breach and do not write a permanent test against `app.py` for it; the bug is in the hook and @Redline owns that file. Where to go next, now that the lock itself has survived 5200 ops across two runs: - **Reset racing increments.** `reset_value` takes the same lock (`app.py:40-43`), so interleaving is safe, but the *accounting* across a reset is where a real lost update would hide. - **A rejected reset must not move the value** (R-1-003) — out-of-range, wrong type, `true` as the value, a malformed body — fired concurrently with increments. - **Replays racing their original** at higher concurrency than 50. - **Keep-alive desync**: @Builder drains the increment body without parsing it, so try an oversized or truncated body on a reused connection and see whether the next request on that socket is still read correctly. @verifier — your NEEDS_WORK on the exit code is **correct** and I am upholding it; gate 2 is red and a red gate is not a GO, whatever the cause. Counter=291 but 311 increments acknowledged (20 lost) 2. Counter=1057 but 1062 increments acknowledged (5 more lost during later operations) ### Root Cause The global lock (`threading.Lock()` at module level, line 24) is not protecting `_value` under concurrent load. Concurrent increment operations are racing: multiple threads are reading the same `_value` before any write back to it, causing updates to be overwritten. The lock acquisition/release cycle (`with _lock:` in `increment_value()` line 35) is not serializing access correctly under 1300+ concurrent operations. Possible causes (for @builder to investigate): 1. **ThreadingHTTPServer connection backlog** — default `request_queue_size=5` may not handle sustained 1000+ concurrent connections (though all requests did reach 200, so likely not the primary issue) 2. **Lock contention with ThreadingHTTPServer internals** — the server's own thread pool might be saturating or interfering with handler execution 3. **Fundamental race in handler invocation** — multiple Handler instances might not be sharing state correctly, or the lock object itself is not thread-safe in this context ### Code Defect (app.py lines 28-43) The lock implementation is structurally correct (global lock, `global` keyword in functions, lock held during increment). The defect is that it does not work under the load specified in R-1-044 (5-second latency ceiling under 1000+ concurrent increments). This breaks R-1-044 and the stage 1 core invariant. @builder — gate 4 exposed a critical race condition in the locking strategy. The global `threading.Lock()` is not sufficient to protect concurrent increments at the specified load (R-1-044: 1000+ concurrent ops, 5s latency ceiling). Investigate: (1) whether `request_queue_size` needs to be raised, (2) whether a different synchronization primitive is required, or (3) whether ThreadingHTTPServer is the wrong choice for this workload. @planner — this is a BLOCK that must be fixed before re-run.   42. Builder→ @adversary, @planner, @redline, @verifier2026-10-04 11:25:42

    @verifier this BLOCK is reading a log that's already been correctly interpreted — it's the same file, `evidence/gates/s1/N1-2-g4-20261004T111204-7fb5.log`, and line 5 of it reads: ``` mid-storm the counter is 291 but 311 increments have already been acknowledged from 0; an update was lost mid-storm the counter is 1057 but 1062 increments have already been acknowledged from 0; an update was lost invariant: True — ``

    Show the full message

    ` `invariant: True` is the settle-time check — it re-reads the final counter and compares it against every accepted increment's return value. A **permanently** lost update would still be visible there; it isn't. The two `mid-storm` lines are the storm's `transient()` watcher firing *while ops are still in flight*, not the final verdict. `RESULT: FAIL` on the summary line is gate 4 reacting to `transient`, which is exactly why plan/s1-status.md (section "Reading a gate-4 log") says the verdict must quote `invariant:` verbatim rather than the summary line alone — because the summary line can't distinguish a real lost update from this. This is the identical misread the status doc already records and overturned once: "Two seats (@Adversary, then @Verifier) independently misread this as a service race, and the second one issued a BLOCK recommending @Builder replace a correct `threading.Lock`... Overturned at 9ca6d72." @Adversary pinned the actual cause empirically: it reproduces at **concurrency 2** (ruling out lock contention), and traced it to `hook.py`'s `transient()` reading the server value before the client's own `accepted` counter — a torn snapshot in the *test*, not a race in `app.py`. I independently reproduced the same thing from three different server architectures, including a lock-free single-threaded one, before seeing any of this land — a bug that shows up with zero possible thread interleaving isn't a locking bug. @planner's own "Verdict discipline" ruling applies directly here: unchanged evidence (same log, same commit 9ca6d72) shouldn't produce a new verdict, and a verdict shouldn't oscillate. Nothing to re-investigate on my end — the lock and `ThreadingHTTPServer` are correct and I'm not replacing them; the fix is `hook.py`'s sampling order, which is @redline's open T1 item 2. @adversary for visibility since this directly concerns your finding.

    Show the full message

    lanner) — all HOLD, no change from last round Reset-racing accounting at scale (15 rounds × 200 increments racing a reset, exact pre/post-reset decomposition checked): 0 violations. Rejected-reset-under-race at scale (10 rounds × 150 increments racing 50 malformed resets): 0 violations. Replays racing their own original (10 rounds × 400 identical concurrent requests): 0 violations once I fixed my harness to catch transport errors as data instead of crashing — see below, because at this width the *transport layer* is where it broke, not the lock. Keep-alive desync with a legitimately fragmented (slow, correctly-Content-Length'd) body on a reused connection: handled correctly, connection stays usable. (A separate attempt where I lied about Content-Length — declared 5 bytes, sent 68 — did desync the next pipelined request into a 501, but that is the client violating HTTP framing, not a defect in this service; any stdlib HTTP server does the same. Not filing that one.) ### The real finding: concurrent *new* connections, not concurrent requests on pooled ones Every test so far — mine and gate 4's own hook — reuses a connection pool (`httpx.Client` with keep-alive). Stage-1 l.15 and R-1-044 say "1,000+ concurrent operations" with no such restriction, and a real storm of distinct clients opens distinct TCP connections. I tested that directly: **1,000 concurrent brand-new connections, each sending one `POST /counter/increment` with `Connection: close`, no pooling.** On a freshly started container: **162/1000 (16%) never got a response within an 8-second grace period** (actual latencies up to 13.2s), and the rest were slow too — TCP connect-time alone had p99 = 5.1s. This reproduced on every clean run I tried (not a fluke of a loaded host — I re-verified against a fresh container specifically to rule that out). Root cause, exactly as @planner flagged: `ThreadingHTTPServer` inherits `socketserver.BaseServer`'s default `request_queue_size = 5` — the TCP accept backlog — which `app.py`'s `main()` never overrides. At 1,000 simultaneous SYNs against a backlog of 5, the kernel queue overflows and connections queue or get dropped, well past R-1-044's 5-second ceiling. Gate 4's `hook.py` uses one pooled `httpx.Client` per worker thread (max 128 connections total per `_new_client`), so it structurally cannot exercise this path — this is why gate 4 passing (modulo its own unrelated `transient` bug) gave no signal here. The fix is the one you already named: raise `request_queue_size` on the `ThreadingHTTPServer` instance in `app.py`'s `main()` (e.g. to 1024+), which is a one-line change. I did not make it — that's @Builder's file.

    Show the full message

    increments have already been acknowledged from 0; an update was lost mid-storm the counter is 1057 but 1062 increments have already been acknowledged from 0; an update was lost invariant: True — RESULT: FAIL — invariant broke mid-storm 2x (first: ...) ``` **`invariant: True` is in your own log, right after the two lines you quoted.** That's the settle-time check — taken when nothing is in flight, comparing the final counter against the exact count of accepted increments. If 20 updates had really been lost, that line would say `False`. It doesn't, on this run or on any of the other three `N1-2-g4-*.log` files from today — all four say `invariant: True`. Other threads finish increments in that gap, so `accepted` is always sampled fresher than `value` — `value < accepted` is the expected result of that ordering, not evidence of a lost increment. It scales with throughput (worse at higher ops/concurrency) and disappears if you sample `accepted` *before* the server read instead of after — I reproduced that fix independently: 3000 ops at concurrency 50, sampling order corrected, 0 violations. I then ran three more targeted attacks the planner asked for specifically to stress the lock (reset racing increments, rejected resets racing increments, replays racing their own original at width 400) — 70+ rounds, 0 violations. The 8 s timeouts themselves were against *my* `/tmp` reference, a thread-per-connection stdlib server that genuinely cannot take 1,000 simultaneous fresh connections — against @builder's real service they may not occur at all. But the leak is structural and independent of that: **any** test that abandons in-flight writes poisons everything after it in a shared-service, single-process gate 2 run. It only needs to time out once. For @adversary to fix, not me — I have not touched the file. It needs to drain or await every connection before returning, so it cannot leak increments forward.   89. Adversary→ @builder, @planner, @redline, @verifier2026-10-04 11:43:14

    @builder @verifier @planner @redline **FIXED — commit `c7c4615`. Good catch, confirmed and repaired both directions.** @Redline, you were right and the mechanism you named was exact: `_send_one`'s `except OSError` branch returned without closing `sock` on a timeout, and — more importantly — the 8s abandon window gave up on connections the server might still be about to accept and process. The server increments uncon

    Show the full message

    ditionally once it reads the full request, with no regard for whether the client is still listening, so a request the test gave up on could still land arbitrarily later, during whatever test ran next. Fix: every connection is now waited on to a genuine conclusion (success or a real transport error) via a 60s hard timeout, closed in a `finally` either way, before `_send_one` returns. The 1,000 connections still run concurrently (`ThreadPoolExecutor`), so this doesn't serialize anything — it only stops the test from moving on while a request is still in flight. Pass/fail still judges each latency against R-1-044's real 5s ceiling (`LATENCY_CEILING`); only the "how long do we wait before giving up" knob (`HARD_TIMEOUT`) changed, from 8s to 60s — generous past the ~16s worst-case delay I'd seen, so a slow-but-eventually-successful request is waited out, not abandoned.
